Because the least we can say is that things have been dragging. And this is in particular due to the delay with which the government tabled its adjustment plan in Parliament. To a point where there is now traffic jamming. Indeed, the new report of the Monitoring Committee is already announced, which should set the scale of the effort to be made for the 2017 budget in the context you know. This is the context set by both the National Bank and the Plan Office, which consider that in order to achieve balance in 2018, the government will have to find 8 to 9 billion euros by 2018. We warned you, you didn't want to listen. Your risky management of tax revenues, your unfunded tax shift - it must be remembered that your tax shift is not financed up to 50% - and your chronic denial of the Court of Auditors' remarks put you in a very difficult situation today. We also learn in the press that you could postpone your budgetary conclave for 2017. And so the trajectory to 2018-2019. You would take it, we always read in the press, the Brexit as a pretext. This is particularly worrying and does not suggest any good for the rest of the year. But there, too, nothing does, you keep your 460 million. The tax on intercommunals is an ideological measure. The MR needed an election trophy. They got it with the tax on the intercommunals. In the end, it will be people who will pay more, but obviously, it has not prevented the vote on this measure. In doing so, you have adapted the device, you have recalibrated it a bit, but what tells us that it will be enough in the eyes of Europe? What tells us that this carat tax will also be able to be a solid measure that will generate reliable performance, regardless of the moral and ethical problems that this poses? We have exposed them widely here, and I will not go back to that. In short, these EUR 50 million are also hypothetical. Another point is tax regularisation, which is still not voted on. This is indeed a blockage of a very high amount, since the Court of Auditors' report estimated it at 7.4% of the compressible expenditure. In other words, when you extract from the budget all the incompressible expenses for which you have no room for manoeuvre, there is one volume left. And the 960 million block represents 7.4% of this volume. This is enormous, especially since some departments, quite rightly, are exempt from this blockage. We can only welcome this, but it means that the share that will be carried over to the other departments is only greater. ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u118	Mr President, the report of the Monitoring Committee incorporated the update of revenue and expenditure on the basis of the new macroeconomic parameters which themselves emerged from the February economic budget. As usual, 153 million technical corrections are taken into account. Following these corrections, measures totalling EUR 1.6 billion were decided. I'm not going to go back to the menu because we talked about it at length together. When we take into account the most recent technical corrections and transition parameters that ensure the transition from the nominal balance to the structural balance, we can deduce that the balance of Entity I is expected to reach 1.6% in 2016, which corresponds to an improvement in this structural balance from year to year of 0.6% of GDP. This result also enables European requirements to be met in terms of improving the structural balance while integrating the expenditure associated with the major challenges we face, the asylum crisis and the expenditure related to the fight against terrorism and radicalism. I had the opportunity to explain in committee that all these expenses were well integrated into the balances, both the nominal balance and both the structural balance, and this explanation was corroborated by the Court of Auditors' statement that these expenses are part of the balances. The flexibility clause is something else. The flexibility clause will intervene if there is a deviation from the budget path. It will intervene in the analysis, in the assessment that the European Commission will make of this potential deviation. It is therefore an ex post analysis, after the fact, after expenditure, which is done by the European Commission on our budgetary path. The call for the flexibility clause if necessary does not prevent the full inclusion of these expenses in the balances. This should be clear to everyone. The Court of Auditors' report was one of the starting points of our discussion in committee. The role of the Court of Auditors is to take a critical look at expenditure and revenue and at the new measures affecting them. This is, and always is, a classic and recurrent exercise and the recommendations made should be seen as beacons to help the government implement these measures and achieve them objectively. The Court of Auditors has indeed made comments, but it has also indicated that it is becoming increasingly well informed, allowing it to make more specialised comments. I certainly do not want to put the criticism aside, but I would also like to underline the words of the representatives of the Court of Auditors, who said during their hearings in committee that there were clearly less inadequate appropriations than usual and that no serious problem was identified. This demonstrates the seriousness of the government's approach. As I pointed out in committee two weeks ago, I remember that in future we will have to give Parliament more detailed explanations of new and complex concepts in order to avoid misunderstandings with the Court of Auditors and Parliament. I am thinking in particular of the use of the flexibility instrument, because we note that there are still difficulties in understanding this, but also the transition between the nominal and the structural balance. In that perspective, I would like to say that we have used the same methodology as usual and as in previous years, and it is the first time that the Court of Auditors has made a comment on the difficulties for Members to understand this. We accept that and next time we will give a better explanation of the passage on the nominal and structural balance, but I repeat that we have used the same methodology as usual. I have also heard some of you refer to the problem, in their view, of the lack of transparency that would result from the inclusion of provisions in the budget or blockages made through underutilization. I have already had the opportunity to respond clearly to these two remarks, but that will not prevent me from answering you again. I would remind you that underutilization is lower than in 2015. In that year, an underutilisation of 960 million had been found, while the Court of Auditors, at the time of the initial 2015 exercise, had pointed out what it considered to be a priori potential credit deficiencies. On the other hand, this year it also points to some overestimations of expenditure. This brings me to the way in which under-utilization is constituted. There is indeed a natural under-utilization because one cannot spend 100% of the budgets, for several reasons: delays in delivery contracts, cancellation of public contracts. And it is true that this natural under-use comes in addition to the under-use decided by the government, embodied in administrative blockages of credit. I would point out here that the use of blockages is not new since it has been used under other governments. I would also like to draw your attention to the fact that these blockages are subject to a few exceptions in order to be consistent with the policy pursued by the government. They are clearly stated in the circular. There's Justice, OCAM, Crisis Center. You must also know that the departments themselves were able to propose a breakdown of the blockages on their own budgets, taking into account the priorities they had set themselves. Moreover, by definition, a blockage can be lifted quickly in the event of a problem. With regard to transparency on blockages, Mr Dispa himself referred to the circular annexed to the committee's report. In addition, the Parliament has all the blockages that have been submitted to agreement. As far as the provisions are concerned, in my view, they meet the criteria referred to by the Court of Auditors. Provisions are, in fact, appropriations that are set aside for a specific purpose, but the final allocation is not known. Thus, within the framework of asylum, there was indeed still a significant uncertainty about the needs of FEDASIL in the coming months, depending on the evolution of migration flows, but also on the needs of the SPP Social Integration, which will encounter the first effects of asylum seekers who arrived in 2015. In the case of asylum in particular, the increase in the appropriation through the provision also allowed the department to quickly have the means to implement projects that fit within the priorities set, without waiting for the appropriations to be adjusted in a budgetary adjustment. This same logic also applies to the provision of terrorism and radicalism in the sense that projects are approved as they progress. As far as the control of these expenditure is concerned, their use is subject to strict criteria of budgetary surveillance, whether by the Finance Inspectorate or myself and in any event, their use is limited to what they have been constituted for. In addition, credits are granted only when the amount of the expenditure is certain. This adds to the strict control of expenditure. As regards visibility and access to information by parliamentarians, I would point out that all distribution orders are published in the Belgian Monitor, which is available everywhere. Mr. Dispa had a specific request for reporting. I guess it's of interest to others too, isn't it, Mr. Laaouej? The Act of 22 May 2003 states: "The Minister for Budgets shall periodically transmit to the House of Representatives and the Court of Auditors the statement of appropriations, as well as their allocation by programme and by basic allocation". This exercise is conducted quarterly and the next report will be sent to the House in mid-July. Finally, there was one question on the general accounts. ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u119	The Minister for the Budget has painted the general picture of the budget and has also addressed a number of aspects relating to expenditure. It goes without saying that we have to do budgetary control and budget layout in a bit of special international circumstances, but I will talk about this briefly later. Questions were asked about the revenue items. As regards VAT on electricity and, more specifically, the research carried out by the ISI on abuses, the report of 24 February this year on the ISI surveys shows that the amounts of VAT which may be recoverable from the large and medium-sized distributors amount to EUR 41.5 million instead of the EUR 250 million foreseen during the initial preparation of the budget. The files are being processed with the ISI and follow the process set out in the application procedures. As regards regularisation, as was agreed here last week, there is an opinion on the substance of the Council of State. We are also working on cooperation agreements, specifically with Flanders. We hope that this will be done with Wallonia and Brussels as well. Also, within the FPS Finance, ten additional staff are planned for the Contact Point Regularisations at the Prior Decisions Service through internal mobility. Further preparations, including on ICT, are under way. Finally, with regard to regularization, I would like to comment on the amounts involved. In 2015, the Regul finally raised 78 million instead of the 184 million registered at the October review. In 2016, the proceeds of the new regulatory legislation were registered at 250 million. So that's 172 million, 250 minus 78, extra compared to 2015. This explains the different amounts circulating around the regularisation process today. As far as the Cayman tax is concerned, it should certainly not be forgotten that a significant proportion of the revenue involved in the legal arrangements endorsed by the Cayman tax is movable income from shares, accounts and the like. This is in principle subject to withholding tax. With regard to the overall yield of the Cayman tax, in order not to complicate the tax return excessively, we have not introduced any additional codes. Without these additional codes, it is effectively more difficult to estimate the specific yield of the caiman tax. We will also regularly evaluate the caiman tax, as is the case with a new tax. If it turns out that escape routes exist, we will of course not hesitate to close them as soon as possible. For ISOC on intercommunals, these are mainly difficulties in terms of reservations, which have prompted some intercommunals to address the Constitutional Court. In the meantime, an amendment has solved most of the problems. The original 2014 law, which in some cases excluded intercommunals from the tax of legal persons, aroused a lot of resistance, because several issues had not yet been resolved. The 2015 Act addressed these issues, including the issue of reserves and built-up provisions. This should remove the objections of several intercommunals and also convince the court to consider the proceedings they have instituted. With regard to the new tax measures, there was also a discussion about the real estate vehicles. In this respect, we expect a recurrent inflow of EUR 250 million each year. It is true that the composition of these 250 million euros differs in the short term and in the period in which the vehicles are at cruising speed. The Court of Auditors' comment on the real estate vehicles is about the one-off conversion of an existing stock to projects to the new statute in 2016 and part in 2017, which in 2016 will once pay 116 million euros of exit tax and once again 50 million euros of exit tax in 2017. From 2018 onwards, it is about exit taxes on the 2 billion euros of new projects that we expect to be implemented annually. One has the recurrent exit tax on those 2 billion euros of new projects, each year, and a one-off effect of the exit tax on the existing stock that was introduced in 2016 and part in 2017. The tax regime for the CFPF has been aligned on the existing tax regime for the GVV. There is therefore no new tax regime in place. Then I come to the Panamanian measures. I see Mr Calvo is gone. It is true that there are a dozen measures, some of which cannot possibly be estimated from the budget. Nevertheless, these measures will certainly have an impact. It is for caution's sake that we have finally entered gross EUR 67.8 million, net EUR 65 million of income from these measures. As regards the Diamant tax, the "diamant" scheme was laid down in the programme law of 10 August 2015. To the extent that the "diamond" scheme is sector-specific, the entry into force of the scheme has been postponed until confirmation by the European Commission that the scheme does not constitute inadmissible State aid. Although the Government remains of the view that the "diamond" scheme, as provided for by the Programme Law of 10 August 2015, does not constitute incompatible State aid and adequately addresses the control problems identified, a limited adaptation has been made to the scheme and presented to the European Commission following discussions with the Commission. With this adjustment, the scheme is even more specific, even more focused on a solution to the particular problems in the monitoring and recovery of stocks, which is relevant in the context of the finding that this measure would not constitute irreconcilable State aid. I will then come to the VAT and bet and the amount of EUR 39 million. Contrary to what the Court of Auditors is asking, there is indeed a clear legal justification. Firstly, the principle of fiscal neutrality is respected. The available data on the gambling sector suggest that consumers using online gambling and money games make up a very different audience than those doing it offline and, in other words, opt for the physical gambling and money games. For example, 88 percent of the traditional audience involved in gambling and money would never play online. The fact that the online market and the physical market are previously separate markets is also reflected in several opinions, including from the Belgian and French competition authorities. This market segregation results, inter alia, directly from the different circumstances in which those services are provided. Online services can be consumed in particular 24 hours a day, which is obviously not the case for offline services. As far as the sharing economy is concerned, the government's proposal will bring income to the surface that was not previously taxed. Indeed, revenues from limited by-activity are often not reported. There is also a need to maximise costs, so that net income falls below the limit for social security contributions to be self-employed in the secondary occupation. Witness the various investigations and corrections, specifically in the sector of services offered via online platforms. Moreover, the complexity of the regulation often led taxpayers to shift behind the argument that they did not know whether and in what way these revenues should be declared. However, under the new system, the tax authority is informed of the nature and size of the additional activity of a taxable person through the fact sheet obligation. The speculative tax is currently being analysed, an analysis that will be discussed immediately within the government. The only thing I have said about this so far is that, on the basis of various informal contacts with the sector, it appears that the budgetary objectives may not be met. More importantly, there is a negative impact on, for example, the stock market tax. I believe it is my responsibility as Minister of Finance to follow up on such developments and to make alternative or corrective proposals in good time. Finally, I have a comment on the general framework in which we find ourselves today and its impact on revenue. As a result of the higher than expected growth in the fourth quarter of 2015, both the Planning Bureau and the National Bank have not recently adjusted the growth forecast for 2016. Indeed, this higher growth in the fourth quarter of 2015 has a beneficial effect on annual growth for 2016, which for the time being compensates or neutralizes the negative impact of, for example, terrorist attacks on the economic climate. However, in its latest prognosis, the National Bank makes it very explicit that the months-long threat of terror and the very unfortunate attacks of 22 March will not miss their effect. They will take their toll. For this purpose, the National Bank puts forward a minimum of 0.1 % of GDP, which is EUR 400 or 500 million. In particular, the Brussels catering sector suffers from the clearly decreasing influx of foreign tourists and business travellers. Tourism revenues in general have fallen sharply. We also see this very clearly in the turnover figures, in the VAT returns of the catering industry, in particular in the Brussels Region. There is no contradiction between higher than expected economic growth... The fact that growth for 2016 is left unchanged has everything to do with the last quarter of 2015. There are economic circumstances, and I would like to say nothing about the Brexit, which unfortunately mean that we have to be less optimistic about the environment.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u138	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Health Committee discussed the draft law at its first reading on 24 and 31 May. The draft was discussed at second reading on 14 June. This draft was also requested by the government as a matter of urgency. The discussion of the draft began with Mrs Onkelinx's question to organise a hearing. It pointed to a difficult dossier, on which there should preferably be a broad consensus. Mrs Van Camp, acting President, reminded us of Rule 28 of the Rules of Procedure, which provides that, in the absence of unanimity, a majority vote must be required to decide on the organisation of a hearing. Mr Thiéry asked the Minister whether the draft had been subject to prior consultation with the sector. Mrs Muylle also pointed to the history of origin. In 2012-2014 there were round tables with the sector and then there was no consensus. At the time and now, the Minister has decided to make progress and submit a draft. It is important that the design can start in September 2016. The Minister then handed over the list of persons she had heard in the preparation of her draft. The committee rejected Mrs Onkelinx's proposal to organise hearings by 11 votes to 3. Subsequently, the introduction was given by the Minister and the general discussion was opened. Mr Hufkens reminded us of the long process of this dossier. Quality progress is being made in the area of health care. On behalf of her group, it is obvious that psychotherapy should be evidence-based. The question was also asked whether the Minister was also considering regulating the profession of clinical sexuologists. The colleague also stressed the importance of the law coming into force in September 2016, as provided for in the coalition agreement. The honourable Member is also asking for an early repayment, including in the context of the future budget. Mr Onkelinx reminded us that when she dealt with the dossier in her capacity as Minister of Health, she worked hard to take everyone's opinion into account. Finally, a compromise for minimum training, supplemented by a theoretical and practical internship, was adopted. The present design does not follow this. Mrs Onkelinx also considered the reservations expressed by the Council of State and concluded that the draft was simply unacceptable. Its vision is purely corporate. The draft comes back to an agreement, a compromise, obtained after many years of discussion and months of work between majority and opposition, between Dutch speakers and French speakers, between the actors in the field. In her view, the text shows an unimaginable lack of coherence, it does not take into account the reality in the field and it will not, under any circumstances, allow the patient to be protected effectively, as was possible under the 2014 law. Ms Muylle recalled the creation of the 2014 law. The committee then decided to proceed in a phased manner and agreed to the compromise text. The Committee considered it important to create a legal framework for the reimbursement of mental health care. The design implements the principle that psychotherapy can only be performed by a doctor, clinical psychologist or clinical orthopedagogue. However, many bachelors are doing a great job today in mental health care. Mrs Muylle also stressed the importance of very broad transitional measures and of the perspective offered to the bachelors, including the prospect of cooperation between private institutions, colleges and universities. Ms Somers stated that she too felt it was important for the law to enter into force effectively in September 2016. According to her, the point of contention in the debate is the positioning of psychotherapy. The present draft does not recognise psychotherapy as a separate profession, but rather as a form of treatment which can be applied by persons who have already received a certain initial training, have acquired a professional title and also have a recognition and visa. According to Mrs Somers, the integration of psychotherapy into the law of 10 May 2015 offers the necessary guarantees in terms of quality. It also considers the transitional measures to be very broad. Mr Jiroflée pointed out that the 2014 law wanted to respond to an influx of psychotherapists, but that that aspect was completely destroyed. The transitional measures envisaged by the Minister do not change that. She also says that her group is concerned about the quality of care to which the patient is entitled. That quality of care was guaranteed by the law of 2014. By restricting access to the profession of psychotherapist, not only reduces the diversity of therapeutic approaches, but also reduces the supply of care. Mr Jiroflée also mentioned, among other things, that the present draft law seems to have been tailored to certain professions in order to ensure that certain persons are no longer allowed to practice psychotherapy in the long term. Mrs Dedry pointed out four different points of the draft and also expressed four reservations. She stressed the importance of the legal basis for the psychotherapists, with a view to reimbursement. It also welcomed the broad transitional measures and the minimum skills required by the draft law to separate the chaff from the wheat. The bill also provides a place for psychotherapy in health care. However, Mrs Dedry had doubts about the seriousness of the proposed minimum standards for access to the profession. She also supported Mrs Muylle's remark that the bachelors who had prior medical training should be able to benefit from transitional measures. Mr Caprasse wondered why the Minister abolished the psychotherapist's profession and reduced psychotherapy to treatment. She found it incomprehensible that some members were against the law in 2014 and are now supporting this bill. She also addressed the training. According to the design, it is sufficient to have a master's degree in clinical psychology or to be a doctor. She also wondered what was the place of the sexuologists in the design. Mr Thiéry felt that every effort should be made to protect the patient. This means that the care provider has received training that meets scientific requirements as well as patient expectations. In 2014, the MR Group already encouraged the approach to include psychotherapy as a type of treatment reserved for clinical psychologists, orthopedagogues and doctors. Mr Thiéry pointed out that his group has always held the view that the training in psychotherapy is a specialised competence, with the most natural access route either a master's degree in psychology or a course in medicine. Mr Thiéry was also pleased that the training would be given to universities and colleges. Mr Senesael referred extensively to the opinion of the Council of State of 29 March 2016. Mr Gerkens regretted the process in which the actors concerned were not heard and the debate was therefore extended. In her view, the draft law does not provide additional protection for the patient. Furthermore, Mr Gerkens stated that the psychotherapy training as such does not exist and that the training as such is a sub-training. Educational institutions primarily train practitioners, such as doctors, clinical psychologists and clinical orthopedagogues. In her view, the basic training of a general practitioner is not sufficient to start training in psychotherapy. She also criticised the composition of the Federal Council for the mental health professions and shared the opinion of colleague Onkelinx after reading recent scientific literature. Mr Janssens limited himself to two questions relating to the composition of the Federal Council. After a long debate, the Minister replied to the various questions by clarifying once again the objectives of the draft, inter alia that the draft law aims to maintain the diversity of treatments and that therapeutic freedom is safeguarded. The mental health care should be included in the co-ordinated law of 10 May 2015 on the exercise of healthcare professions because the patient needs to be assured that his provider has received quality training and that the quality of care will be monitored. The Minister concluded by replying to the comments made by the Council of State. Mrs Gerkens took the second round by saying that it was unacceptable for her to change the law for universities because they refused to organise the bridge programme. She also stressed that reimbursement for mental health care should be regulated. According to Mrs Gerkens, the draft drastically reduces the number of healthcare providers whose interventions are reimbursed. Ladies and gentlemen, we will then have the article-by-article discussion and the vote, without a final vote, which took place on 14 June 2016. The committee then considered a second reading of the draft. Mr Senesael then suggested that the profession of psychotherapist was not recognised. He insists that the legislator ignores the wealth of properly trained professionals. He also considered that the Federal Council for the mental health professions was not sufficiently representative. Ms Fonck started her speech by agreeing to the objective of providing patients with added value in terms of quality. Mrs Fonck also felt that it was a good thing that attention should be paid to access to health care. It does, however, have comments on the patient's offer of care and on the transitional measures. Finally, Mrs Fonck also stated that she was concerned about the impact of the design on the structures employed by psychotherapists, because those practitioners were losing their autonomy. In his speech, Mr Frédéric discussed the acquired rights and the protection of the title of psychotherapist. He states that by not providing for an authorisation or recognition, the legislator does not provide sufficient protection for the patient. Mrs Gerkens also points out from the first reading report that many questions remain unanswered, including questions about the concrete consequences of the Minister's position on psychotherapy and on the title of psychotherapist. It also considers that the Minister's replies on the concrete definition of supervision are not sufficiently clear. There were also too few answers to questions about the possibilities of reimbursement, the reference frameworks, training centres and the composition of the Federal Council. Minister De Block stressed that she remains of the opinion that psychotherapy is a form of treatment that is part of the psychological treatment. Anyone who provides psychotherapy as part of the design will also be able to be called psychotherapist. There is also a need to ensure maximum protection for patients and access to care. According to the Minister, she wants the design to combat quacking and sectarian excrement. The Federal Council should be set up as soon as possible, as it plays an important role in drafting the implementing decisions. The Minister ends the debate with the fact that a pacification is indispensable. Hence the broad wording contained in the transitional measures. The whole bill was adopted by 10 votes to 3 and 2 abstentions.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u140	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, in addition to the report, I would like to briefly express our views on this dossier. Let me be clear, I think we are taking another important step today, as in 2014. It is an important step to create the necessary safe framework for the patient-client who is in an extremely vulnerable situation. Ladies and gentlemen, we are well aware of the context. There is a huge increase in psychological care in our society. We prescribe too much medication, we treat too little, we have too long waiting lists and the financial thresholds are often too high. The reimbursement must be made quickly, but this cannot be achieved without the recognition of care providers and a strong organisational model of our mental health care. This draft is a first important step in this respect. It amends the 2014 Law on some fundamental points that prevented the implementation of the 2014 Law. In particular, it concerns the procedure before the Constitutional Court of the orthopedagogics, the impossibility of organising the bridge programme and the complexity that arose in delivering the authorisations for the psychotherapists as a result of the sixth state reform. Colleagues, the comments of orthopedagogists are reflected in the draft by introducing a minority opinion in the Federal Council and adding a diagnosis to the definition. These questions were also on the table in 2014, but due to the very different educational landscape in both parts of the country and the lack of a full master's degree in French language education, it was then impossible to integrate this. The influx into psychotherapy is only made possible for the masters in the future. Of course, I was outraged at first reading, because we made other choices in 2014. Through the bridge programme we saw the bachelors influx into psychotherapy. I have also seen how people have struggled over the last year to achieve this bridge programme. If this doesn't work, then I think choices need to be made. I also see the advantages of the containment of psychotherapy in KB nr. 78, which was not possible in 2014 because the Minister had only once decided to open the KB, only for psychologists and orthopedagogics, and we were right to seek a solution outside the KB. I have always asked for three points of attention throughout the debate. A first point of attention is the appreciation for those who are doing brilliantly on the work field today. The competences acquired must therefore be able to be honoured, they must be able to be evaluated. The broad transitional measures contained in the draft today give that guarantee, whatever one may say. The additional condition that non-health appeals should cooperate with other health professions is also strong. This is not a negative discussion about autonomy. For me, it is a positive discussion about cooperation, networking and multidisciplinaryness of our care, which must strengthen for all care providers and which is the only right way forward for the future. My second concern is also to give a future to the many bachelors who see their future in mental health care and who cannot or do not want to take the first step towards a master's level. With the supporting GGZ profession we give them a very nice perspective there and we also fill a need in the field, which is a lot of demand. I therefore ask the Minister to make quick work, together with the Communities, of the supportive mental health profession. Thirdly, I would also urge that the access of the masters should not be interpreted too narrowly and that the list should also be expanded quickly. I am thinking, among other things, of the Masters of Psychic Nursing and the Sexuologists, which is also made possible by the design. Further work on practical placements, both for graduate psychologists and in therapy training, is also positive, as is the incantation from 2017 of the course of therapy within the field of education, by allowing them to work together with colleges and universities. Colleagues, training for recognised health professions or actions should therefore, in our view, be encased in the field of education. Colleagues, is this design ideal? No. Does it deserve our support? Yes. It will also be important to ensure that the many royal decisions, Madam Minister, are carried out quickly and in terms of content. Will there be any troubles on our way? Yes, because for that the field of work is complex and the dossier is complex, but let us solve it together. I would therefore like to thank everyone, both ministers, their collaborators, colleagues from the majority and opposition, for the long way along in the dossier.
ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u141	Mr President, Madam Minister, ladies and gentlemen, part of our society is in a state of suffering. She's morale in the dark, obviously I'm not teaching you anything. The latest check-up of the Belgian health system, carried out by the KCE, the INAMI and the Scientific Institute of Public Health, shows this, the number of suicides is high, the consumption of antidepressants continues to increase and care in psychiatric institutions is constantly increasing. As we know, there is a great variety of psychic sufferings: depression, burn-out, mal-being are increasingly common. And if the feelings of anxiety, impotence, loss of benchmarks, and self-confidence that result from them must be taken care of, they must obviously not be taken up solely by the use of medicines. Of course, drugs are effective in treating certain mental pathologies and disorders, but they are not in all cases, on the contrary. The management of certain psychic sufferings through speech, dialogue, communication is indeed essential, beneficial to the patient. It's the very essence of what we call psychotherapy. To cite just one example, I will talk about dealing with depression. According to a KCE report published in 2014, psychotherapy combined with antidepressants should be the preferred option in this context. And if the patient in major depression does not want combined treatment, it is psychotherapy that must always be offered as the first choice, because it is at least as effective in the short term as drugs and is more effective in the long term. A psychotherapy performed by a quality practitioner therefore offers chances of healing and well-being. And reading the scientific literature, what characterizes a good psychotherapist, is his ability to adapt, to be flexible. It is indeed this reactivity by which the psychotherapist adapts his intervention to the patient and to his particular situation for one purpose: to create a relationship of collaboration, help, trust in order to maximize the effectiveness of his intervention. I would also like to pay tribute to the work of these many professionals, who today provide effective and indispensable assistance to those who feel the need for it. In this field, one must be able to remove one's eyes, to undo one's prejudices, to open one's eyes, to listen, to look at one's full interest in this specific field of psychotherapy, at the crossroads of medicine, psychology and the social sciences. Psychotherapy is complex and should not be considered merely a medical act. To make my point clear, I would like to give a precise example of Siegi Hirsch, the 2002 Mensch Prize. It is recognized in many countries as one of the precursors of family therapies in Europe. Jewish of German origin, it was in a concentration camp that he forged his idea of the individual, his ability to change and the importance of the imagination as a refuge against barbarism. First of all, as an educator in children's homes, he was only later educated as a social worker. To help an adolescent in difficulty build an identity, he has given priority to an approach related to how his family works. In France, Siegi Hirsch shared his experience of therapeutic work in institutions. He trained educators, psychologists, children's judges. Without knowing this, he also conveyed his approach to hundreds of psychotherapists from the family, the couple and institutions in Belgium. He has trained trainers. He trained an impressive number of psychiatrists. One of the psychiatrists said of him that "when he takes care of families of deportees, Siegi encourages them to remember positive anecdotes that go in the direction of life rather than to work by reemerging trauma. It was through his thought, his work as a teacher and a psychotherapist that he contributed to the work of memory." It is a reference that must lead us to reflect. This example allows us to observe that from a life experience and serious practical training, this man has been recognized throughout the world as a reference in psychotherapy. Today, with the bill that many are about to vote, Siegi Hirsch could not be recognized as a psychotherapist. Let's not face it! In addition to experiences like this and high-quality psychotherapists, there are also charlatans. Drifts exist, as do harmful interventions and abuses. For several years, some parliamentarians, in particular, have considered it necessary to frame the practice of psychotherapy for one single purpose: to protect patients. In the end, two trends emerged very quickly: on the one hand, those who believe that the diploma must take precedence and, on the other, those who believe that it is training that is essential. Is it really necessary to see things in such a dichotomous way in this complex field? While I was Minister of Health, it was without preconceived opinion that in close cooperation with the committee's parliamentarians, the majority as opposed, we had decided to take up this problem with arms. A complex but fruitful work which had led to an important consensus between these trends, which I have just mentioned, between the majority and the opposition, a consensus which corresponds to the different sensitivities between the North and the South of the country, between the actors on the ground and between the different currents. The aim of this law was to protect the patient by guaranteeing him/her trained and experienced professionals to help him/her. It was also an important step forward for practitioners themselves, clinician psychologists, orthopedagogues and psychotherapists, who were thus recognized and protected through professionalization of their practices. This text was certainly not perfect, like many. He doubtless deserved some adaptations and clarifications: a programme law and implementing decrees would have remedied them without difficulty. Instead, Madam Minister, you are totally questioning the compromise and, it is true, you have been supported by parliamentarians who have completely returned their jackets. I have in mind what Mrs Muylle was able to say at the time, who left in the meantime, who had been an ardent defendant of this conception of this law and who today pleads exactly the opposite.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u143	Intervening to say that he's going to intervene is interesting! We've been gloating a lot about what psychotherapy is compared to medicine. Why in terms of psychotherapy, should we be able to deviate from the logic of the evidence? Medicine seeks an adequate response to suppress a symptom, a treatment that suppresses a cause. Psychotherapy, for its part, mobilizes the resources of the person, of the family, of its network to increase its freedom of choice. There is no one-size-fits-all solution. The causes of ill-being are either exogenous, so we look for what power we can have over these causes, or endogenous, then we will seek to restructure the way in which the person thinks, feels, acts. The work is done in a relational process and in a specific epistemological framework. This definition makes it possible to understand why, in this matter, it is questionable to want to work as if it were a medical act like another on the basis of all this reflection on the evidence. Increasing the focus of care will, moreover, inevitably lead us to an objectivation of mental illnesses. So it's a bias on your part. I regret, of course, that this opinion has not been followed up on this and many others. For these reasons, it is very important that patients with mental health problems receive appropriate, safe and quality assistance. Due to a hugely fragmented landscape, in which almost everyone can call themselves a psychotherapist, patients unfortunately become a play ball between different therapists before receiving appropriate help. In times when suicides increase, that's disastrous! Despite differences of opinion on the two sides of the language border, the draft law no longer makes it possible for anyone to call themselves a psychotherapist. By a clear definition of psychotherapy as a treatment form and by the clinical psychologist and orthopedagogue in Royal Decree No. 78 to record, charlatans will no longer be able to hang a sign of "psychotherapist" at their door. Our group is therefore very pleased, Madam Minister, that you have provided for very broad transitional measures for those who are not going to exercise a medical profession but are able to work as a supporter in a mental health profession. Moreover, the offer will not be reduced. The draft law still allows for the opening up of the profession to other disciplines, such as clinical sexologists. In short, for us, the draft law creates the necessary clarity and opens the perspective for people who have had experience in the field for years and for newcomers who want to enter psychotherapy. In addition, this is also a great achievement for the first time between the different disciplines. It connects the whole field. Moreover, the Minister has assured that additional measures will follow which will only improve the obligation of referral and interdisciplinarity. We welcome that. I will conclude, fellow Members. The design that is on the table is not perfect, but it is a beautiful design that eliminates the impossibility of the previous text, takes into account the sensitivities on the ground, both on the Flemish side and on the French-speaking side, and puts the most important quality and safety first. Our group therefore fully supports the draft and considers it extremely important that it can enter into force on 1 September 2016, as provided for in the coalition agreement, if only because the draft law does not mean the end. The draft law and the clear definitions of those who can exercise psychotherapy will make it possible to examine the reimbursement of psychotherapeutic consultations. We really need to start this discussion, especially since mental health is becoming increasingly important in our society.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u181	Madam President, Minister, a lot has already been said, of course, so I will keep it fairly concise and stick to the essence. The concern for psychological suffering has become an increasing challenge in recent decades. It is the core task of a government to define the framework conditions for providing quality care for patients with mental health problems. The contribution of this draft cannot be underestimated. The design creates a robust framework for the recognition of care providers in mental health care. Contrary to what some fellow Members say, after the entry into force of the law, there will still be a wide range of healthcare providers. First of all, we have the regulation for the future. Clinical psychologists, clinical orthopedagogic doctors and doctors who have received an additional training in psychotherapy will be able to apply psychotherapy in the future. In addition, we provide for particularly wide transitional measures for those who work today, for those who study today or in September start their studies in psychotherapy and even for those who engage in pre-training as a professional within the meaning of the law of 10 May 2015 or another bachelor's programme in September. Those who are professionals will be able to exercise the profession of psychotherapist autonomously. Anyone who has another Bachelor's degree as a pre-training course will exercise his profession under the responsibility of a psychotherapist. The draft law also provides for the possibility of admitting other professionals to psychotherapy training. So there is room for even more diversity. Completely new is the fact that we provide the legal basis for creating supportive mental health professions. In this way, in future, bachelors will also be able to take their place in the care of people with mental illness. We see that doctors and dentists are today asking for support professions because the workload is too high. The same applies to mental health care. There, too, there is a great need and, therefore, the demand for support professions which can carry out specific tasks. So I am not afraid that these support professions will not be there. From the opposition, diversity is often confused with quality. In physical medicine, we all find it obvious that there is evidence-based work, that the training is sufficiently robust and that there is a hierarchy in the tasks of the caregivers in function of their obtained diploma. That's what we're going through in psychic medicine. Patients with mental needs are also entitled to mental health care based on evidence-based practices. This design therefore strengthens a number of quality conditions. The design strengthens pre-training for psychotherapists because it is essential that the psychotherapist has a broad knowledge and can therefore recognise underlying problems and refer them appropriately to care providers with another specialty, including colleagues with a specialty in physical health care. The design introduces the professional internship for clinical psychologists and clinical orthopedagogics because we believe that there is insufficient practical experience in the current training to provide quality care. We introduce a staged system of who may or may not practice autonomous psychotherapy, taking into account pre-training. Moreover, working under responsibility does not require the physical presence of the person responsible for the therapy. It should rather be seen as a multidisciplinary cooperation. We introduce psychotherapy into the law of 10 May 2015. This provides the government with the necessary tools to take the charlatans out of care. Clinical psychologists, clinical orthopedagogic doctors and doctors are caught by the law on healthcare professions and are therefore also subject to the control and sanction possibilities of this law. In this design, we no longer mention the currents within psychotherapy. We think that in a health care domain that is still in full development, it would not be wise to record currents in a law and therefore to pin care providers on a flow. Based on the patient's needs, the psychotherapist should be able to apply those techniques that will have an effect on the patient, regardless of the flow to which the technique belongs. Moreover, the accusation that private institutions should no longer be used is not correct. By concluding cooperation agreements with colleges and universities, they will be included in mainstream education. In all honesty, I can only see benefits for these private institutions. Cooperation offers an important opportunity for upgrading and allows them to offer their students recognised diplomas. The cooperation between colleges and universities and private institutions should ensure that the future psychotherapist receives both theoretical and practical training that will enable the patient to receive high-quality care. This draft also solves some of the practical problems that have arisen with regard to the Federal Council. From three guesses we go to one council with three banks. We provide for the possibility of a minority position and solve the problem of the absence of clinical orthopedagogues in Wallonia by working with clinical orthopsychologists. It is a pity that some have made this subject a Community debate. However, I note that there is no such contradiction in the faculties of medicine in Flanders and Wallonia. That's where reason clearly won over the emo. I therefore conclude that this design draws the map of the patient in mental health care. Based on the patient's right to a high-quality mental health care, the design creates the necessary conditions for training for clinical psychologists and clinical orthopedagogues and for the pre-training and training of the psychotherapists. This dossier has a long history in Parliament. We are finalising the legal framework today. Now the way is open for the debate on reimbursement.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u183	Madam Chair, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, earlier I interrupted Mr. Thiéry by saying that the bill that we are going to be working on is a particularly important subject. Not because a law passed at a given time must always remain the same throughout the years, but because it was the result of 15 years of reflection, of work, of sometimes conflicting relations between different actors, between different currents. And if we got to that law, it was because we managed to identify the elements that could bring us all together around quality stakeholders, to help people in a psychotherapeutic process. Fifteen years of participatory work that led to a law. I am not going to take up all the elements of this law but, nevertheless, some, of which Mr Thiéry I think, said that the white card of the twelve university professors proposed that, even more than a master, one had to be a doctoral student in order to be a psychotherapist. In the 2014 law, we required that we have a bachelor's degree, a further bridge training of at least one year at university level, and at least four years of theoretical training, practical training and supervision. It's much more than a master's degree and it's almost the equivalent of a doctorate. I cannot therefore accept the arguments that the quality and training requirements of the 2014 law were insufficient, that they need to be strengthened and that it is for this reason that we are creating a new law. The arguments that make sense, even if I do not share them, are that we are going to make a selection of the people who are going to give these trainings and that the emphasis will therefore be on very specific actors, namely universities and, to the point of rigour, higher schools, and on how this training will be given. In any case, this is not a better quality, a better level and better requirements. The other argument used to advance a necessary change in the 2014 law is that psychotherapists were actually recognized but not included in the law that contains the list of health professionals. Clinician psychologists and orthopedagogues were there but not psychotherapists. I would like to recall the precautionary principle that guided this alternative. We were, at the time, with professionals already trained, already recognized, who were doctors, psychiatrists, clinical psychologists, orthopedagogues. Then we were with a new category of stakeholders: psychotherapists, about which we put requirements in terms of skills to acquire, in terms of personal development to carry out, in terms of number of hours, days of supervision and training in the field. And we have assigned advisory bodies to determine what the criteria could consist of, which we would use to determine whether a person had acquired the skills. These skills were clearly detailed. Training centres could be universities, colleges and specialized centres. But also in this respect, we had to determine precisely the requirements we had for these training places. It seemed to me legitimate to say – and this still seems to me legitimate – that it was up to the councils composed of university professors, the representatives of the profession and the therapists trained in the various disciplines to define these requirements correctly and precisely so as to be able to accept and recognize psychotherapists and centres. As long as it was not done, it was prudent to recognize them as professionals but to include them later in the list of health professionals. This did not prevent the reimbursement of mental health care, an argument put forward now to amend the law. Another argument is that it is thanks to the introduction of psychotherapeutic services in the law of 2015, formerly royal decreed in 1978, that things are possible! In the 2014 law there were positive and interesting elements, arguments to say that we had to change it that I cannot share in view of what I am developing here. With regard to the transition, it is said that there was no transitional phase in the 2014 law. It was organized differently than what is provided for in the current law. But there was a case-by-case review of each on the basis of criteria that would have been defined. On a case-by-case basis, an assessment would have been made as to whether the candidate was appropriate, whether he or she had acquired the required skills, whether the training had been completed in a centre, or whether he or she needed further training. Not everyone was recognized as ex officio, but a path was allowed so that those who did not meet the requirements could comply with it. It was therefore not necessary, Madam Minister, to change the spirit of the law to meet the concerns that you say you want to pursue today. You can change it because you have another philosophy, another orientation. It is your right and I criticize it, but the arguments put forward to say that it was necessary to do so are in any case not valid for the examination and analysis of this 2014 law. Another element is that no one wanted to set up the bridge, no one wanted to set up this academic year or this year that allowed to acquire the skills, skills and prerequisites necessary to be able to begin specific psychotherapy training. It's a little upside down. There is not always consistency in the attitude towards institutions and universities, which sometimes provide training that requires recognition. This is still the case with orthopedagogues which existed only in a Community before being recognised. Your law makes it possible to assimilate clinicians, orthopedagogues for a period of time, etc. In fact, creating confusion. Osteopaths are a problem because some universities do not want to do this kind of training. So we don't recognize osteopaths. And then, here, because universities don't want to organize a bridge year, we don't recognize and change the whole system. If there was a clear position on the conditions, they would have adapted their program. In fact, many universities are able to follow course modules and complete basic training, even if it is true that some do not respect Bologna or the fact that someone who comes from a high school and has another training to complete can access a master's year or a completed training without requiring him to redo the whole course. But we must not enter this game if we want training actors and psychotherapy actors who are correct and have a societal vision and of general interest. What we put in the law was serious. It's swept away like it doesn't make any sense anymore. The title of psychotherapist as a professional title is withdrawn. There will be no more professionals who can call themselves psychotherapists. There is recognition of the doctor, recognition of the clinician psychologist, recognition of the clinician orthopedagogue. And then, they will specialize and apply a specific treatment from time to time, which will be called psychotherapeutic treatment. Where's the logic? Where is the logic when high-quality training is required? Where is the logic that the most qualified is ultimately not recognized as a professional? There's something I don't understand either. You focus on basic training and consider that it is basic training that will ensure that the psychotherapist will, yes or no, be a quality psychotherapist, rather than putting the emphasis, all the energy and all the demands, in this specific training, that must be long and accompanied by a personal development work. Because the psychotherapist obviously cannot manipulate the person facing him. He cannot be trapped in the relationship, in relation to his own history and personal development. This is where the quality requirement of the future psychotherapist must be important. And I regret that these quality guarantees are not included in the bill. This confusion between basic training and specialized training in quality requirements is likely to cause damage. In the same way, among the requirements mentioned in the law, without any clear definition, there is a reference to internships, places of approved internships, rather than keeping practical training organised by a training institute (university, specialized institute or other). The training process would involve supervision, going back and forth between the field and the analysis of what happened, reflections on how the psychotherapist lived and analyzed the situation, returning to the field. These are elements that are foreign to an internship. It is really high-level vocational training that must be done by combining an approach of another type. And in your project, you confuse an internship with this approach. On the other hand, you say in the law that "by-laws will define the criteria for traineeships and traineeships". I really wish you good luck in identifying this if it is not linked to a formative approach and training institutes. In the arguments that you put forward to justify the interest of this new law, we can join you on the possibility of paying more for mental health care. It is true that the needs of people who are unwell, experiencing difficult times, suffering abuse or suffering at work are becoming increasingly acute and are not alien to the functioning and way of organizing our society, our economic life and the requirements of normalisation vis-à-vis citizens. Your law does not include a refund mechanism. So we don't know what will be refunded and how high. On the other hand, by reading your project between the lines, we understand that, for the benefit to be possible and eventually reimbursed, it will take a journey of care. This will be based on a single method. At times, you have expressed yourself by specifying a number of benefits for such situations experienced by people in difficulty. It is therefore another approach to health care, which uses the possibility of reimbursement to organize them in a way that conditions people to have to respect what is expected of them in exchange for care. Another way to seduce or, in any case, pass the pill is through the methods you have chosen to recognize current providers during the transition phase. I will not repeat all the criticisms that have been made. If I wanted to be a cartoonist, I would say that almost everything will be possible to those who are already exercising. I regret that, whatever their way of working, they will be able to continue to exercise. It is not that, to organize a transition phase by guaranteeing quality and fighting against those who would dishonestly practice. A transition period is a provision that first recognizes the quality of the claimants and then allows them to continue their activities. Here, it's quite different. Mr Thiéry's argument is that those who do not have basic training in the health professions will be placed under supervision and will not be autonomous. This is the guarantee given to patients. At the same time, this supervision will not imply that we will follow it all the time. He will have to work in multidisciplinary teams. Nor is the manner in which this will be done detailed. Obviously, it is interesting to look at each other from different backgrounds and approaches. But this is not the way to guarantee the psychotherapist's autonomy of functioning and to guarantee quality care. Here, too, there are confusions, references to modes of practice that are alien to the content of the text and which are a little to show that quality is aimed at regardless of the requirements of quality of training and psychotherapy. I very much regret the abandonment of references to the four currently recognized frameworks: psychoanalytic and psychodynamic orientation, behavioural and cognitivist orientation, systemic and family orientation, and person-centred and experiential orientation. It's impoverishment. As things evolve, there may be another frame of reference, but considering that the current references are outdated and that a good psychotherapist will be effective, able to draw from each orientation and perhaps from other things... and to systematize his approach, is to banalization, an attempt to normalize people that goes against a psychotherapeutic relationship where one must identify with a person the path that he can take to enter the path of change that he wishes. It's a co-built relationship all the time. We'll be far from it if we follow what comes out of this bill. What we were referring to in the 2014 law was to allow the person to acquire these skills. He stressed the need to identify and recall the diversity of frameworks. He didn't make up fifty. He took over the four recognized executives and indicated the interest that there might be in associating his basic training and psychotherapist training in the name and recognition of the psychotherapist so that the person who consults him knows exactly who he is addressing. This additional door, of course, is also so that we can make the difference and we can recognize that there are differences between charlatans and psychotherapists who exercise with quality. For, basically, today, the bill does not differentiate between charlatans and psychotherapists who do not enter into the new conditions to practice independently, since tomorrow it will be "supervised" to be able to continue its activity. And since supervision is not defined, it opens the door to any kind of so-called supervision. I therefore propose that an individual examination be carried out of the file, of initial training, of continuing training, of expertise, an examination of the experiences in contact with the patient, also in relation to multidisciplinary practices. As a reminder, many psychotherapists who will no longer be able to practise in autonomous practice tomorrow work with a multidisciplinary approach. In this way, all those who have trained and have this expertise in contact with the patient would see the quality of their work recognized and would be respected. I know some of them who have practiced psychotherapy for twenty or thirty years, as I reminded you right away through an example. It is a minimum in terms of respect for their competence. This is to avoid, Madam Minister, in the event of a serious situation, such as a therapeutic accident, major legal procedures being initiated because no one has defined who assumes civil or criminal responsibility. Letting this be blurred – something that I have never seen in terms of health care organisation – and not basing this supervision on any legal basis risks provoking legal procedures tomorrow. ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u206	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to begin by thanking all the members of the Health Committee for the more than thorough discussions for more than twelve hours in two sessions, which we have devoted to this draft. I would also like to thank Mrs Muylle for her more than thorough report on those two readings. I have also pointed out on several occasions in proposing this draft law that the 2014 law has taken a major step forward in the recognition of the healthcare professions concerned. However, some adjustments were needed to implement the law. This is my responsibility to make additional adjustments to ensure the patient's quality of care. What's positive for the patient? What has that law changed for the people on the ground now? After seventeen years, there is the long-awaited but above all necessary recognition of clinical psychologists and clinical orthopedagogues. That's about almost 20,000 people on the premises. There is also the recognition of doctors, who, subject to additional training, have chosen to do so as a health care profession. This ensures a more quality mental health care tailored to the patient's needs and needs. Of course, this will go hand in hand with better accessibility. The existing visibility of these recognised groups in the field of work, the high level of expertise and the daily professional commitment they give for many years without recognition is now finally being validated. The definition of the competence of clinical psychologist and clinical orthopedagogue is entirely in line with the existing care models in the facilities, both federally and within the Communities. It also gives the various governments a chance to really think about how they can pursue a more accessible affordable mental health care. Without recognition, we can't go to a refund. This is a next step. The next step is the reimbursement of mental care. We have already invested a lot in mental health care. That was also necessary, because that was a very underrated terrain. We will continue to do so and in the long term we will seek the means, if everything is settled after this law has been passed. The law also finally provides a framework for support professions in mental health care. The provision of the law allows professional bachelors, who are currently outside the law on the exercise of health care professions but in the psychosocial domain, to be given a place within mental health care. This is a real valorisation for all kinds of people with titles that have been touched here, all kinds of bachelors in the sphere of psychology and pedagogy. Indeed, the law lays down, and Mrs Muylle has pointed out, very broad transitional measures for persons who are already in psychotherapy and even for students who are studying psychotherapy. By way of derogation from the principle that psychotherapy can only be exercised by a doctor, a clinical psychologist or a clinical orthopedagogue, the design provides for very broad acquired rights for pre-existing psychotherapy practitioners. I have raised all these positive points to say, like some Members, that it is actually a law designed to improve the quality of mental care for the patient. I think that's why it's all about. Changes have indeed been made to the original design. That was necessary, because there was, among other things, a procedure before the Constitutional Court, which was rightly instituted by the orthopedagogics. That's what we remediated. Amendments were also needed, because the Federal Council, as provided for in the original text, was not at all applicable. The parity could not be guaranteed because there are no titles of orthopedagogy in the French-speaking part of the country. That Federal Council is very important, because it will have a lot of tasks. We want to put it together very quickly. In addition, psychotherapy remains an important part of the patient's treatment. She is given a specific place in the law on the exercise of health care professions, which at the same time makes psychotherapy more valuable. In the past, the profession was not recognised, but the practitioner was authorized. What was an authorization, however, was not known and could not be given. Training was described in the form of switching programmes, but it has never been put into practice; specializations have been described, but universities have never been able to offer them. Now we choose more evidence-based mental health. Evidence is becoming increasingly important for mental health care. One can make treatment choices based on available scientific information taking into account the patient's question and the expertise of the treating therapist. The fact that the law on the exercise of health care professions did not apply to psychotherapy was contrary to the whole of the reform, some of which you have implemented, Mrs Onkelinx. I place psychotherapy within the law on the exercise of health professions, where it belongs. She remains an important and well-defined part of psychology, pedagogy and psychiatry. So she is what she is, a treatment form. That is also said by the Knowledge Centre. Indeed, we have not followed all but the most important recommendations of the Supreme Court. We have consulted a great many people. The text is also in line with European directives. Moreover, the Council of State did not make any comments on this matter. I have already said that it is a good thing for the patient, because it will have to respect the law on patients' rights. The provincial medical committees will be able to take effective action against practices that harm the patient. They couldn't do that before. Accessibility improves as well as the quality of care. Mr Hedebouw, when one comes to someone, one can trust that he has the right skills. So the chaff is separated from the chaff. As Mrs Hufkens stated, for more than one reason, the 2014 law was not applicable. It's been remediated. With regard to the discussion about supervision and whether or not it can function autonomously, it is clear that we do not really see supervision as a purely physical relationship. That has been said several times in committee. The supervision can also mean that a patient was referred, that a staff meeting is held about a case or that there is a feedback. That is the reality, Mrs Fonck, on the ground. Multidisciplinary work is already under way. If you say there is no such thing, you ignore reality on the ground. It has also been said on several occasions that final responsibility always lies with the supervisor because he has to ensure that the law on the exercise of health care professions is properly respected. It is a surprise to me that the opposition wants to cause so much concern on the ground and is opposed to a huge improvement in the organisation of mental health care. Some of us have worked hard to do that in the past. But it can happen. Mrs Somers stressed the added value of this draft with regard to the quality of health care and cooperation in the field of education in institutions, colleges and universities. Mrs Muylle also referred to this. This cooperation does indeed provide academic support. That wasn't there before. If an institution provides an education and there is no link to a college or university, there is no academic support in that training either. This is essential because psychotherapy is also an evolving form of medicine. This design rightly puts the patient at the centre of attention. When I hear that there is fear and uncertainty, I ask what fear and uncertainty there was in an area in which seventeen years of hard work were worked by all people and in which there has never been recognition of the fact that clinical psychology, psychotherapy and orthopedagogy were in the health care sector. Mrs Gerkens, you said you were going to be a caricature. That is your right, but I think this is far too important to make a caricature of it. The relationship between the aid worker and the patient must, of course, always be professional. There can be a lot of emotion and empathy, but it must always remain a professional relationship and everyone knows that. What are additional values for the patient? In addition to improved accessibility, there will now be continuity of care. There will have to be continuity of care and there will also be waiting services where the patient can go if he suddenly feels worse. In the long run, there will be a refund for psychological care. How can one achieve better accessibility than by recognising these professions which have been delayed for far too long by all kinds of circumstances? When I came to this Chamber in 1999, people were already talking about it. That was 17 years ago. I would have been delighted to have seen that this could be arranged much earlier. I believe, Mr President, that much has been said and a great deal has been built up. A lot of mistakes have also been made. For example, Mr Hedebouw has referred to a letter from 250 associations, whereas it is actually about a letter from 250 professionals. I am not putting that letter next to me, but I have not read any new element in it. Many untruths have also been found in the mailboxes. I can understand that some people would rather treat this like spam. The patient does indeed have the right to go to a well-educated care provider. I believe that this draft law has been a real step in this direction. I thank all those who have made a constructive contribution. I believe that this is an important step forward.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u219	Mr. Speaker, Minister, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, this bill is part of our government's desire, the majority of us, to enhance the social status of self-employed workers, who are, we must acknowledge, the true engine of our country's economy. Within this framework, this bill focuses on women's entrepreneurship as well. This bill contains three main provisions. The first is an exemption from social security contributions for self-employed workers, who interrupt their work following childbirth, without affecting social benefits, namely family allowances, sickness and disability insurance and pension. The second provision is a new legal basis for responding to a series of earlier Council of State opinions on a range of other bills in order to ensure legal certainty for provisions promoting the reconciliation between the professional and private lives of self-employed persons. There are therefore three things: first, helping the self-employed who have just given birth, in order to help them resume their professional activity, in this case a home helper of a household nature. Second, the adoption allowance also for self-employed workers. And tertio, the allowance of close assistant for self-employed workers who interrupt their activity in order to provide care to a close person seriously ill or to their disabled child. The third provision concerns the updating of various provisions to refer to the insurance applicable to maternity care.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u233	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, the reason for my initiative is the forthcoming NATO summit in Warsaw. The two-yearly NATO summit of Heads of State or Government will take place on 8 and 9 July. This summit is important because, every two years, NATO clearly focuses on the current problems and challenges. Today, this NATO summit is incredibly important because the security situation in Europe is changing. On the one hand, we have the Russian threat on the eastern flank and on the other we have the new threats and threats south and central. Every day there is an attack somewhere in the world. Yesterday it was in Turkey. Turkey was hit by a very serious terrorist attack. At Istanbul Airport, dozens of people were killed and injured. I would like to express our condolences to all these victims and their families. Of course we condemn these cowardly and barbarous acts. Turkey is a NATO ally and, like the other countries, we will also show our solidarity with the Turkish people. I should like briefly to point out that the attacks are very similar to the attacks we made three months ago. That means a lot. This resolution, ladies and gentlemen, naturally also seeks to strengthen Belgium's focus and to clarify our country's role in this changing security environment. What's it gonna take? Firstly, I believe that it is important that the strategic plan for defence, which is actually the compass of these and subsequent governments, should be definitively laid down by this summit within a good week. As you all know, diplomatic and military circles are looking forward to this plan with great interest. Ladies and gentlemen, this is in line with the Christmas agreement and with the commitments made in Wales two years ago. I'll come back to that later. This government has been the first government in 25 years to break the negative spiral when it comes to financial resources and budgets, and invests heavily in security instruments again. Of course, defence is a very good example of this. Thirdly, this resolution also calls for our commitment and the strengthening of our contribution to NATO operations. There is no need to stress that we want to remain a credible partner at international level and that we want and will play our role in this respect too. We will therefore deliver our fair share. That is why, ladies and gentlemen, we are in favour of a good balance between homeland security and other tasks.
