I would like to take this opportunity to thank our military for their efforts over the last few weeks and months. They carry out a difficult task in difficult circumstances. The balance between homeland security and international missions and foreign operations must not, of course, be compromised. In the resolution we give an example of our contributions in Afghanistan and in the Eastern European countries. Fourthly, as far as cyberspace is concerned, there have been many cyber attacks in recent weeks and months. I therefore believe that cyberspace needs to be given much more attention within the NATO alliance than is currently the case. The resolution before us focuses on a number of issues, as I have just said. Finally, we call for a pragmatic approach, a consensus, to be adopted in relation to Russia. On the one hand, we condemn Russian violations. You know that these violations have been piling up lately. On the other hand, of course, we want to continue our dialogue with Russia in order to bring about a normalization of relations between the West and Russia. In this spectrum, I think it is important for our country, Belgium, to play its full role as a bridge builder in diplomatic circles. I'll come to my decision. We are a NATO partner, but of course, in addition to strengthening our presence, we do not want to separate our role within NATO from enhanced cooperation with the EU, the UN and, of course, other regional organisations such as the OSCE. I hope that this resolution tabled by myself and my fellow Members from other majority parties will also benefit from the support of our fellow Members from the opposition.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u297	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, as part of the preparation of the 2016 budget, the government had decided to devote an envelope of 50 million to correct the impact of the tax shift. This allocation was allocated in equal shares, on the one hand to increase the purchasing power of beneficiaries of social integration income, on the other hand to increase the smallest pensions. That is the second aspect we are dealing with today. In fact, within a defined budgetary framework of 25 million, the government has decided to allocate this amount to the revalorisation of the lowest pensions, both for private and self-employed pensions. In this context, the rise in the lowest pensions of 0.7%, which corresponds to more than eight euros per month at the rate of isolation, is a strong signal to our pensioners who need it most. This measure will also strengthen the link between the career provided and the amount of the pension. For this year, the pensioners concerned will therefore receive a catch-up premium in December. And as of 2017, this revalorisation will be paid monthly. This is a kind of social pension bonus, as the Minister pointed out, which will complement the measures taken in the context of using the 2015-2016 welfare envelope. In addition, the King will be empowered to provide for an increase of up to 10 per cent and to ease career conditions up to 43/45th or equivalent fraction, if he considers it feasible. Before concluding, I would like to add that both the Council of State and some of my colleagues, including Mr Daerden, have questioned the penalizing and discriminating nature of this measure vis-à-vis women. It seems to me important to recall that, on the contrary, this measure will be all the more beneficial for women pensioners in the private sector, since it takes account of similar periods. But it must be borne in mind that, while many women have lower pensions today, the problem does not come from pensions but is at the level of careers. It is precisely at the career level that discrimination must be addressed. Finally, on the issue of consultation, let me regret that a unanimous opinion could not be delivered. However, this measure is deeply social and, thanks to it, more than 150,000 pensioners will once again see their pension increase. It's unfortunate, but that's how it is. However, as the Minister has already done, I call on the social partners to get involved in the near future and to make proposals for the allocation of the welfare envelope 2017-2018 to raise the lowest pensions. In conclusion, Mr President, I would like to say that, yes, we have abolished the pension bonus which benefited a small part of pensioners for an amount of EUR 18 million to grant it to pensioners who need it the most up to EUR 25 million. It is a choice made by the government that we fully support because it is a deeply social project.
ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u298	Mr President, Mr Minister, ladies and gentlemen, of course, this bill provides for an increase in the lowest pensions, but this increase is so small that it fails to compensate for the other restrictions on purchasing power that affect these small pensions. I would like to recall that 55 per cent of women meet the minimum pension requirement, compared with 94 per cent of men. So, even if the minimum pension amount was significantly increased, which is unfortunately not the case, it would not solve the problem of women who fail to qualify for it. Of course, the discrimination of women against the pension is not recent and can be explained by several factors. They often have an incomplete career and pay 20 per cent less than men. There are 44 per cent of women workers who are part-time compared to only 9 per cent of workers. As a result, many of them struggle to have a career that is sufficiently full to be able to access a decent pension. The 2015 reform has aggravated discrimination, as extending the retirement age to 67 years without corrective action is still making conditions more difficult for women. This project further widens the gap between those with a full career and others, since it provides for an increase of 0.7% in the minimum pensions granted for 45 years of actual work or similar periods. They will still face the greatest difficulty in accessing this meagre improvement in the minimum pension. There is still no corrective action, although one could, for example, have hoped for compensation in terms of the right to a minimum pension. The Council of State gave a critical opinion before concluding: "This condition, which is unfavourable for women, should be removed. The budgetary arguments cannot convince." For these reasons, and despite the slight increase in the proposed minimum pension, we will choose to abstain.

ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u313	Mr President, Mr Minister, ladies and gentlemen, I wish you good morning as we have passed midnight. Access to justice, ladies and gentlemen, is a fundamental right in our society. It ensures that everyone, regardless of his or her level of income, can bring his or her case before the courts and can, therefore, simply defend himself or herself. Without accessible justice, the laws we vote on would remain a dead letter. This is one of the sine qua nons of our rule of law. Conflict resolution cannot be abandoned to the arbitrariness and the law of the strongest. It is therefore also a guarantee of equality between all citizens. If only the most wealthy had the means to enforce their rights, the rights of the poorest would be hollow concepts. That is why the state has an obligation, Minister, to guarantee and organize this access to justice for all through legal aid. As has been pointed out on several occasions, it is a fundamental public service in democracy, whose access cannot be restricted, even in the name of budgetary considerations. However, nothing is free and the current system is not perfect. We know that perfectly. Already today, many litigants are, in fact, excluded from legal aid, since because of the scales, they are considered too rich to be able to benefit from legal aid when they are in fact too poor to afford the services of a lawyer. In addition, lawyers also face difficulties, as they sometimes have to wait more than a year before being paid. We know, moreover, that the amount they are supposed to receive remains uncertain, to the extent that the field associations denounce the inability to find lawyers who are sufficiently specialized in complex matters. The lawyers' councils believe that the point should reach a value of 75 euros to be fair then, that today it oscillates around 25 euros. The closed envelope, which is the current system, would require that the number of files processed by legal aid offices be divided by three to reach that amount. The moderator ticket will never allow you to meet this request. The only certainty offered by this project is the exclusion of some citizens from effective access to justice, as the moderator ticket will play its role and moderate access to justice for certain segments of the population. The only consideration of this exclusion will be financial and will in no way be related to the quality of the remedy that would be abusive. Yet the moderator ticket is sold to us as a necessity to combat abusive remedies. It would even be these abuses that would explain the annual increase in the use of legal aid. Some social categories are also aware of injustice, for lack of means, but they are only on the defendant's bench and almost never on the plaintiff's bench. Yet, as we know, justice is never stronger than when it is accessible to all and everyone can freely assert their rights there. ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u343	Mr President, I do not think it is necessary for me to read the entire report. I refer to my written report. However, I would like to stress, for the many colleagues who were perhaps not in committee at the time, that three texts have been tabled. We must take into account the work carried out by our colleague Mrs Fernandez Fernandez, by our colleagues Mr. De Vriendt and Mr Hellings, of course, and by our colleague Mr Buysrogge, who is the main author, the first signatory to the proposal to amend the Rules of the House, which was finally adopted by nine votes and one abstention. I wanted to point out that our colleagues had also tabled texts. I'm sticking to the report. I'll just say two words for our group. The MR Group considers this amendment to be important. It concerns the verification and control of the resale of military equipment by the army. This is a case that has often been brought up to date. In particular, between 2002 and 2010, 16 fighter-bombers were resold to Jordan, from frigates to Bulgaria, 166 armoured in Morocco. They are therefore contracts which are not negligible. Between 2009 and 2012, this was the case for 35 demilitarized helicopters and two Airbus A-310 aircraft. The feeling of all who participated in the work was that the regulations were too loose and the controls were too light, even though National Defence had demilitarized the aircraft. But it is clear, and it is a satisfaction, that Parliament will carry out a more in-depth review of these arms resales, including until the identity of a new end-user. It is important to stress this. It also strengthens democratic control.
ParlaMint-BE_2016-06-29-54-plenair-ip117x.u344	Mr President, I would like to thank my fellow Members who are still here for their perseverance in attending this evening's important amendment to the Rules of Procedure. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the regions have for some time been largely responsible for arms trade and arms exports, but the federal government has remained competent for the sale of obsolete military equipment. I would also like to thank the reporter, who also took the opportunity to defend the position of the MR, for the comprehensive report he has come here to present. The army often sells obsolete equipment, ranging from clothing to helicopters, aircraft and armoured vehicles. To give you an idea of the size of the number of sales of military equipment, in 2015 the Defence Department sold 3.93 million euros in military equipment. The export of obsolete or redundant military equipment is therefore not an insignificant fact. In the case of resale of military equipment, all actors must be truly known, not only the final buyer, but also the intermediaries. Until today, however, the Special Committee on Army Purchases was not responsible for such sales records. The present proposal seeks to remedy this by giving the special commission responsible for purchasing the army the power to control not only purchases of military equipment in the future, but also their sale. The proposed amendment to Rule 151 of the Rules of Procedure will allow this. The Special Commission on Army Purchases will then amend its rules of procedure and conclude a new administrative protocol with the Minister of Defence. So there is work on the shelf as soon as this regulation is adopted. Ladies and gentlemen, this amendment to the Rules of Procedure is an important proposal within the framework of our duty, namely the parliamentary control of the government. Here in this Chamber there is therefore a satisfied committee chairman, whose committee power will be extended. Mr President, I would like to end by thanking you. I would like to thank all those who have contributed constructively to the amendment of the Rules of Procedure, both in the Committee on Defence and in the special committee on the Rules of Procedure. I would also like to thank those who tabled a different proposal, in the same spirit as our proposal, but its practical impact differed. I therefore hope that this amendment will be supported in the Chamber.

Since this file has advanced, it is already a success for us who have carried it from the beginning. The text before us today, therefore, is intended to allow for an upstream control by our assembly of the sales of former military equipment that fall 100 per cent of the federal state's jurisdiction, while many cases of replacement of major weapons systems, such as the F-16s, are on the government's agenda. Such a text is essential and fully justified in order to put an end to the current lack of clarity at the federal level. In the current context, this blur was all the more dangerous. The final destination and therefore the intermediaries of these resales of military equipment must be clearly known from end to end, taking into account, in the procedure, the opinions received from the military and/or civilian intelligence service. This better involvement of our Assembly must, however, respect strict rules of confidentiality, which is now permitted by the rules of procedure of the Special Committee on Military Purchases. I shall therefore note, with a certain irony, that our amendments were undoubtedly perfect, since the majority took them on board. However, it would have taken you a year to arrive at this text, which merely gives and moves existing powers already provided for in rule 151 of the Rules of the Chamber to the Military Procurement Committee. Now that this debate has ended on the issue of the Belgian army's weapons, this same parliamentary control will have to be carried out on the police's weapons. I ask that our text, Mr President, soon be put on the agenda of the Committee on Rules of Procedure in order to learn the same lessons from the importance of transparency in this area. Of course, my group agrees in substance with the majority text and will support it and be fully involved in its immediate implementation in the ad hoc committee. For my part, however, I will abstain from protesting against this majority holding-up, hoping, Mr President, that you will not allow the same scenario to happen again.
